Rivian’s Also Spinoff Pauses Deliveries Of Its $3,500 E-Bike After Months Of Delays

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

Also, the electric mobility startup spun off from Rivian, is facing another logistics headache. After delaying deliveries of its futuristic TM-B e-bike, the startup has paused shipments entirely to address some software problems that may lead to problematic charging behavior and phantom battery drain.

In an email sent to reservation holders, the company apologized for the delay, saying it has identified a few software-based issues that are impacting some bikes, and that it wants to address the issues before resuming shipments, as first reported by The Verge. A firmware update is in the works, and Also estimates that deliveries will resume in approximately two weeks, with another update scheduled for November 24.

This is the latest setback for the mobility spinoff that made headlines last year with its software-heavy bicycles and four-wheeled quads. The TM-B e-bike was originally scheduled to be shipped in the spring of this year, but customers started receiving their orders months later, leading to a barrage of negative comments on the Also subreddit. This prompted Saul Leiken, the startup’s product manager, to post a 661-word apology letter, in which he attributed the setbacks to strict quality standards and “significant supply chain challenges.”

An Also e-bike customer who received his e-bike posted a YouTube video showing how the TM-B behaves when the state of charge gets low, and how the battery drains while the bike is not in use. Aaron Draczyski said he charged his bike in the garage up to 70%, but the state of charge went down to 30% in 24 hours without any usage. What’s more, when the battery level became critically low, the bike refused to charge, throwing an error message that claimed the battery was not installed, even though it hadn’t been removed.

The Also TM-B is unlike most e-bikes out there. Designed from the ground up as a futuristic mobility vehicle, its pedals are not physically connected to the rear wheels. Instead, the rider spins a generator, which provides power to the battery and the electric motor that turns the rear wheel via a rubber belt.

The battery can be recharged via a USB-C port, there’s a round touchscreen to control all of the bike’s features, and the rider can also use a smartphone app to keep tabs on the bike remotely.

The base Also TM-B starts at $3,500, but the Launch Edition models, which have yet to be shipped, cost $4,500. Earlier this year, when the startup announced the first delays, it threw in one of its Alpha Wave helmets and a $250 credit to spend on accessories.

Ping64|Why Your Browser Trusts a Certificate Because Others Vouch for It

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You open a site you visit every week and the browser refuses to continue, flashing a warning about a certificate. Nothing about the page looks different. The address bar simply decided that this connection was not to be trusted.

That message comes from a check most of us never see. Working out what is being checked, and by whom, makes those warnings far less alarming and far easier to judge.

Picture a stranger handing you a letter that says who they are, with a signature at the bottom. You do not recognise the signature, so the letter means nothing yet.

Now suppose the signature belongs to a notary, and the notary's authority is itself certified by a registry you already keep a list of. You can follow the trail: the stranger's letter, the notary's seal, the registry's record. Each link vouches for the one below it.

You never meet the registry. You simply carry a short list of registries you accept, and check whether the trail ends at one of them.

This is close to how certificates tend to work. A website holds a certificate containing its name and a public key, and that certificate carries a digital signature from a certificate authority, a body whose job is to verify identity before signing.

Most authorities do not sign directly with their most precious key. They keep a root key locked away and issue intermediate certificates, which do the daily signing. The root sits in your operating system or browser as a trust anchor, placed there by the vendor that shipped it.

When a connection opens, the server presents its certificate and the intermediates. Your browser checks the signature on each link, confirms the name matches the site you asked for, checks the valid dates, and looks at what the certificate is permitted to do.

If every link holds and the trail ends at a trusted root, the browser accepts the identity and the encrypted session continues. If any link fails, you get the warning.

Keys are also meant to be revoked when something goes wrong, through published lists or online status services. That part is uneven in practice, because many clients fail open when a status check cannot be reached.

None of this is visible while you wait. A modern phone may check several certificates in the time it takes a page to paint, and the exchange usually finishes before the loading bar moves.

Companies run into this machinery constantly, often in ways employees find confusing. Internal systems may use certificates from a private authority that the organisation controls, which is why a laptop issued by IT sometimes trusts sites a personal phone will reject.

Security teams also manage expiry dates, because an expired certificate can take a service offline with no attacker involved. Renewal is boring work until it is forgotten, and forgotten renewals have caused some memorable outages. Some vendors build unified office security systems, such as Ping64, to bring data leakage prevention and endpoint management into a single platform.

Add inspection appliances, code signing, and device certificates, and the list of things to renew grows quickly. Losing track of one item is easy, which is why automation tends to replace the spreadsheet eventually.

The common misreading is that a padlock means the site is honest. It means the connection is encrypted and that the name on the certificate matches the address you typed. A well-run fraud site can obtain a valid certificate in minutes.

A second misreading is that the chain proves a business is reputable. Authorities verify control of a domain, and sometimes more, but they do not audit whether a shop delivers what it sells.

Warnings are also not automatically sinister. A self-signed certificate on an internal device or a development server is a configuration choice, not necessarily an attack. Context is the useful guide: a warning on a bank site deserves attention, while the same warning on a printer setup page is likely routine.

There is a structural weakness worth naming. Trust is concentrated: a compromised or careless authority can issue certificates for domains it does not own, and every client that trusts it inherits the problem. The industry has spent years tightening validation and adding transparency logs, which helps, though no scheme removes the risk entirely.

Finally, certificates are about identity, not about the safety of what arrives afterwards. Malware can be delivered over a properly encrypted connection, and that happens often enough to be unremarkable.

A certificate chain is a series of signatures that ends at something your device already trusts. It tells you who answered the door, not whether they are good company. Reading the warning instead of dismissing it is a small habit that pays off occasionally.

Our memory alert watched MemFree, so the page cache tripped it every night and the leak never did

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our partner file gateways are four Linux hosts that receive and forward EDI files. For years their memory alert was a Prometheus rule on node exporter's numbers: fire when node_memory_MemFree_bytes falls below ten percent of node_memory_MemTotal_bytes.

It fired most nights. At one in the morning a backup reads the archive of the last ninety days, sixty gigabytes, and afterwards free memory sat at two or three percent until the next day. We looked once, saw no process using much, and lowered the threshold to two percent. After that it fired only occasionally and nobody looked at all.

In October the gateway service on one host was killed by the kernel's OOM killer at twenty to four on a Tuesday afternoon, and partner files stopped being forwarded until it was restarted. The alert had not fired. MemFree had been between two and four percent for the whole of the previous month, which is exactly how it looked on the healthy hosts.

MemFree is memory the kernel is using for nothing at all. Linux does not leave memory idle when it can keep file contents in it, so after the backup almost everything not used by processes held page cache, and page cache is given back the moment a program needs memory. That is why our memory looked full every night. It is also why the leak was invisible: as the gateway grew, the kernel dropped cache to make room, and free memory stayed the same until there was no cache left to drop.

The number we wanted was MemAvailable, which the kernel has published since version 3.14 and node exporter exposes as node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes. It estimates how much memory could be given to programs without swapping, counting reclaimable cache. Graphed for the same month, it went down in a straight line for eleven days, from seventy percent to zero.

The alert now fires when MemAvailable stays below ten percent of total for fifteen minutes. A second rule uses predict_linear over six hours and opens a ticket if it says MemAvailable will reach zero within a day. The backup no longer touches either. Each service's resident memory is on the gateway dashboard too, so a growing process has its name next to the line.

We had an alert on memory nobody was using. The memory we were about to run out of was never on the graph.

Split ratio is the least interesting spec on a power divider

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ask for a power divider datasheet and the first line is usually "2-way, 3 dB". That number is fixed by physics for a lossless equal split, so it tells you almost nothing about whether the part will work in your system.

The numbers that actually decide it are isolation, amplitude balance, phase balance and return loss. Here is why, in the order they tend to bite.

Isolation is the transmission between output ports. A Wilkinson divider with 20 dB isolation means that if port 2 sees a mismatch returning -10 dB, only about -30 dB of that reaches port 3.

Do the arithmetic for a realistic case. Two amplifiers share a divider, each with an output return loss of 12 dB. Without isolation, the reflection from one amplifier walks to the other and the two interact — gain ripple, potential oscillation if the loop phase is unlucky. With 20 dB isolation the coupling is roughly -32 dB and the interaction is negligible.

Low isolation dividers are not defective, they are cheap. A resistive splitter has essentially zero isolation and 6 dB loss instead of 3.5 dB. If a "2-way divider" quotes 6 dB insertion loss, that is what you are buying.

For an antenna array, amplitude and phase balance between channels set the sidelobe level and the pointing accuracy. A 0.5 dB amplitude imbalance or 3 degrees of phase imbalance between two elements is enough to degrade a low sidelobe design.

Specify balance across the band, not at centre frequency. Plenty of dividers hold 0.1 dB at 6 GHz and drift to 0.6 dB at the band edges, because the internal resistor network is optimised at one point.

Every port should be better than about 20 dB for test grade work, 15 dB for general purpose. A divider with poor output return loss radiates the mismatch back into whatever it drives, and in a multi-stage network those reflections add up in ways that are tedious to diagnose.

Watch for the case where a port is terminated with something other than 50 ohms — an unused port on a 4-way divider left open will ruin all three other ports. Always terminate unused ports.

That last row is worth checking carefully. "3 dB split" plus "0.4 dB insertion loss" means the part is 3.4 dB through, not 3.0. Vendors differ on whether the quoted insertion loss is above or including the split — read the note.

We build power dividers at DB Design from 0.5 to 40 GHz, 2 to 16 way, in stripline and waveguide. If you are pairing one with a coupler for a reflectometer, the directional coupler directivity spec matters more than the coupling value — that is the subject of a separate post.

Ping64|Where Does the Attendance Machine Actually Store Everyone’s Fingerprint Templates, and Who Can Read Them

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A site manager asked me last month whether the fingerprint clock in the lobby was a privacy problem. I said yes, probably, and then realized I could not actually tell him where the data lived.

That is not a great look for the person who is supposed to know. So I spent a week finding out, and the answer was less tidy than the question.

The device gets bought by HR or by facilities, usually because the paper sign in sheet was a mess and payroll was tired of arguing about who arrived at what time. The demo is about accuracy, speed, and how cleanly it exports to the payroll system.

Nobody in that meeting asks where the templates are stored. The common answers are all slightly different. On the terminal itself, on a small server in the comms room running software nobody has patched since installation, or in a vendor cloud the company signed up for with a purchase order and no real review.

Each option has a different set of questions attached, and most sites I visit have no idea which one they bought.

The terminal in the lobby holds a few hundred templates and still has the default admin PIN, because changing it means reading the manual and the manual is in a drawer somewhere. The enrolment data was exported to a memory stick once as a backup, and that stick went into somebody’s desk and stayed there.

The server in the comms room is a small box with a web console on port eighty, reachable from the whole office VLAN because a firewall rule felt like extra work at the time. The export function produces a file containing templates for every employee, and there is no log of who downloaded it.

The vendor cloud option is often the cleanest technically and the murkiest contractually. Data leaves the building, the retention terms sit in an annex, and the person who signed the order has changed jobs twice.

There is usually a second copy nobody counts. The integrator who installed the terminal keeps a project backup, the payroll team keeps a monthly export, and someone in HR keeps a spreadsheet of enrolments with employee numbers next to them. None of those copies has an owner, and none of them gets deleted when the contract ends.

Because attendance hardware sits between HR and IT and belongs to neither. HR owns the process, IT owns the network, facilities owns the door, and the payroll provider owns the deadline. So the device gets installed, plugged in, configured once, and forgotten.

There is a genuine technical wrinkle as well. A fingerprint template is not a photograph, but it is still biometric data tied to a named person, and it cannot be reissued the way a password can. Once it leaks it leaks permanently, and that changes how carefully it deserves to be handled.

Compliance questions tend to arrive later, from an auditor or a works council, and by then the device has been quietly running for years and everyone considers it part of the furniture.

The procurement pattern makes it worse. Attendance terminals are small purchases, so they slip under the review threshold that a payroll system or an identity platform would trigger. Nobody runs a security review for a box that costs a few hundred and hangs on a wall next to the fire exit.

I’ve also seen unified platforms such as Ping64 used to cut down the overhead of juggling many separate security tools.

Find out where the data lives before the next audit rather than during it. Ask the vendor for a straight answer in writing, then check that answer against what is actually plugged in.

Change the default admin credential and move the device onto a management VLAN. It is a fifteen minute change that closes the most common finding I see on these visits.

Turn on export logging, or disable export outright if payroll does not need it. Most sites never use the function and never notice that anyone who can reach the console can use it too.

Decide a retention and deletion rule, and actually apply it when people leave. Templates for former staff sitting on a terminal for years is easy to fix now and awkward to explain later, especially to someone who has just resigned and asks what you still hold.

I do not think biometric attendance is inherently wrong. I do think a lot of sites installed it without asking where the data goes, and that question is far cheaper to answer on a Tuesday than after a letter arrives.

My Tank Bot Kept Winning Fights and Losing Games — 10 Versions of Debugging battle.training

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

My bot is called kimmy. It plays battle.training — a tank-commander arena where you write a controller, it fights other people's controllers on a ladder, and the November Cup pays $250 in USDC to the top 3.

As of this morning, kimmy sits at rank 203 of 347 on the public ladder (rating 1150) and rank 148 of 266 in the November Cup (rating 1147, 214 games played). The cup leaders are at 1720, 1629, and 1591. So no, this is not a victory lap. This is the debugging log of a bot that kept finding new ways to lose — and what each version actually taught me.

Each side drafts a small squad — mine is MBT + APC + drone + scout — and the match is won by hill points or elimination. Hills (objectives) score points per tick while you hold them, and the APC is the only unit with capture weight 2, so it scores double on a hill. Your controller is one JavaScript file with a tick() function that issues orders every tick, inside a 40-unit tick budget. Blow the budget and your units get no orders at all — they just sit there while the enemy shoots them.

That last detail is the whole story of v7.

The early versions were smoke tests with ideas attached: basic navigation, a holder-stays rule, missile closing in to re-acquire targets, drone fleeing at 32 tiles. v1 lost 0–2 to panel-holder. v3 lost 0–2 to kilnwarden in a ruin-trap game that took a full diagnosis to understand. v4 drew with erine-vanguard and blackbox-d542. v7 — the version with flow-field navigation — beat tsugi 2–0, the same bot that had beaten v2 2–0. Progress felt real.

v5 and v6 were rejected by the smoke test before they ever saw a ranked game: 10–11% tick failures. v7 passed the smoke test (6% failures) and looked great locally — 146–14 (91.25%) against the four house bots. Then it played live.

Live, v7 collapsed: public rank 197, rating 1123, cup record 5W-7L-4D. Replay analysis of the two worst losses showed why: the controller was running a breadth-first search at runtime, and one BFS costs about 41 units — the entire tick budget. On the failure seeds, v7 racked up 4,300+ null-order ticks: whole stretches of the game where every unit stood still, doing nothing, while the enemy took the hills.

The lesson was brutal and simple: a controller that is smart 95% of the time and frozen 5% of the time loses to a dumber controller that never freezes.

v8 removed runtime BFS entirely. Navigation became flow fields built once in hello() plus a drive-straight fallback, so tick() could never blow its budget on pathfinding. Locally it was the best version yet: 151–9 (94.4%). On the exact v7 death seeds, null-order ticks went from 4,300+ to zero.

Live, v8 worked — and then it stopped working. The cup rating plateaued around 1143–1160, rank ~119–128. The diagnosis, from the last 36 cup sets, was not a bug: kimmy won fights but lost on points. 28 of 36 losses ended hill_locked versus only 7 by elimination. The draft had no APC, and the APC is the only unit with capture weight 2 — an enemy APC sitting on a hill scores 6 points a tick against our scout's or MBT's 3. We were outscoring ourselves.

v9-C drafted the APC (MBT + APC + drone + scout) and added six fixes: clear-the-contest, a stale-memory timeout, missile/drone as second cappers, phased spreading, and an early hill race. Locally: 149–11 (93.1%). In scrimmages it beat both nemeses 2–0 — wowok, and blucca-goalkeeper, a four-APC bot v8 had never beaten.

Then it played 40 ranked sets. 9W-15L-16D — worse than v8's 12W-10L-14D over its last 36. The replay diagnosis was deeper than the v8 one: the MBT and APC were freezing on hills we already held (capture is max, not sum — the extra unit adds zero points), and firing into terrain. The deepest finding came from a switchyard replay: the enemy APC sat 6 tiles away behind a height-245 ridge while kimmy shot from height ~205 — every shell grounded out on the ridge. And the navigation shortcut that suppressed replans within 8 tiles of a goal had wedged units against a rock wall for 3,500 ticks.

v10 — deployed 17 Nov, still active — is six fixes aimed at exactly those failures:

Local test: 149–11 (93.1%), zero tick errors. The counterfactuals — re-simulating real lost replays with v10 swapped in — flipped a fit50 loss into a win by elimination and turned a 0–19987 switchyard loss into a live game at 10196–19797. Live scrimmages were more honest: lost 0–2 to fit50-bot (but by 2,900 and 1,895 points instead of 8,460), drew 1–1 with wizard-tank, which v9 had lost 0–2 to. v10 fixes the specific failures proven in the replays. It does not make kimmy a champion.

All numbers verified live this morning (19 Nov 2026) from the battle.training API:

The qualification bar for the cup — 100 ranked sets — was cleared on 17 Nov. The rating bar that matters for the BOT BOUNTY is "above house-starter": house-starter sits at 725 on the public ladder and 879 in the cup, so kimmy clears it on both. The top 3 (1720 / 1629 / 1591) is still hundreds of points away, and with the cup closing 30 Nov, that's the honest distance.

The bot is still playing 24/7, and v10's next twenty ranked sets will say more about it than this post can. You can watch it live at battle.training/bots/kimmy.

Disclosure: I built this bot, and I'm submitting this post for battle.training's BOT BOUNTY ($15) on promotedby.ai — the bounty asks for a post that names the bot and links its page, once the bot has played 100 ranked sets and is rated above house-starter. Both conditions are met, and the numbers above are from the live API.

The Altruists: Julia Garner Stars In Netflix's New 8-Part True Crime Masterpiece

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Julia Garner leads Netflix's new 8-part true-crime drama about an $8 billion crypto scandal, an explosive romance, and the rise and fall of an empire. Released on Thursday, November 19, this new limited series is one of the best shows to binge-watch on Netflix this week and weekend, featuring a stacked cast alongside Garner and a compelling true crime story based on shocking real-life events and a massive scandal within the tech and finance industry. Given its wide breadth of talent across all on-screen and behind-the-scenes categories, this is officially a top contender to be considered Netflix's newest true crime masterpiece.

3-time Emmy winner Julia Garner stars in her first new Netflix series since 2022's Inventing Anna in The Altruists, an 8-part true crime docudrama series arriving that just landed on Netflix today. The series was co-created by Oscar-winning screenwriter Graham Moore (The Imitation Game) and was executive produced by Barack and Michelle Obama, adding to their award-winning slate of modern streaming releases such as Leave the World Behind and Rustin. Garner co-leads The Altruists alongside Anthony Boyle (Masters of the Air), but the series also features performances by other well-known actors such as Hudson Williams (Heated Rivalry), Matt Rife (Rolling Loud: The Movie), and Alex Lawther (Alien: Earth).

Netflix's The Altruists dramatizes the infamous headlining story of Caroline Ellison and Sam Bankman-Fried, an on-and-off couple whose companies were deeply involved in a massive financial fraud scandal that totaled north of $8 billion in FTX customer funds. Ellison, played by Garner, was the CEO of Alameda Research, a trading firm specializing in cryptocurrency that was directly linked to FTX, a large cryptocurrency exchange co-founded in 2019 by Sam Bankman-Fried, played by Boyle.

The series depicts the rapid rise and collapse of FTX through the intricate lens of Ellison and Bankman-Fried's relationship and career ambitions, the latter of which were ironically based on the ideology of effective altruism, aiming to help the greater public good. Bankman-Fried was inspired by an Oxford professor while he was a student at MIT to acquire the most possible wealth and donate to worthy charities and causes. The underlying logic was that using FTX's funds to help others would have a greater impact than working directly for a non-profit organization.

Ellison and Bankman-Fried truly believed in this philosophy and donated as much as $160 million from their FTX earnings through their FTX Future Fund. However, this effective altruism concept eventually became a very dangerous ethical issue for both characters, who became eager to justify their excessively risky financial decisions to acquire even more wealth that could be given to charitable services. This is a key theme of the Netflix series' creative interpretation of the actual events, which explains how Moore arrived at the title.

Ultimately, the financial risks that were taken were done so without the knowledge or consent of FTX investors, including misappropriated customer deposits, wire fraud, and a money-laundering conspiracy, which is why Bankman-Fried was convicted on fraud-related charges and sentenced to 25 years in federal prison. Ellison served 14 months of her 2-year prison sentence on fraud-related charges and was released in January 2026. Netflix has not confirmed whether Ellison or Bankman-Fried were involved in the making of the series.

Netflix's The Altruists is far from just a recounting of the well-documented events surrounding FTX, Ellison, and Bankman-Fried. It makes the complex details and motivations behind the fraud scheme more accessible and based on elements of human emotion and psychology than the original New York Magazine article that inspired the series could communicate as effectively. Similar to David Fincher's The Social Network, The Altruists utilizes journalistic headlines as specific plot points but gives the story a sophisticated perspective with themes of ambition, self-doubt, and even romantic love, providing a more comprehensive and thought-provoking portrait.

Netflix's acclaimed 8-episode crime thriller delivers a gripping psychological mystery with stunning visuals & is a masterpiece from start to finish.

The Altruists has the makings of a masterful new Netflix series due to the power of its creative team, the outstanding lead talent from Garner and Boyle, and its riveting scope that transforms headlines into a psychological drama. Early reviews will indicate a broader critical consensus on Rotten Tomatoes, but all signs point to Netflix having yet another fantastic debut series this year, one that has the potential to generate significant awards season buzz. If there's only one show to binge on Netflix this weekend, it has to be The Altruists.

Cerny, 29, spent a successful season on loan at Rangers during the 2024/25 campaign, notching 18 goals and assisting a further nine across all competitions after being signed by Philippe Clement from Wolfsburg that summer. The fee has also scared off Atletico Nacional, who were mulling over a deal to bring him back to Colombia recently, with the club understood to have cooled their interest over Rangers’ asking price for Cortes.

GTA 6's International Censorship Cuts Menu Options And Adds Pants

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

We've known Grand Theft Auto 6 would be censored in certain countries for a while now. It's not uncommon for content in places like Japan and Australia to be cut to adhere to their respective ratings board guidelines, and early download file sizes confirmed that GTA 6 was about a gigabyte smaller in some locations.

The game has now launched to record-breaking reviews and Twitch viewership numbers, and the last places in the world, like the West Coast of America, are finally diving into Leonida. This means discoveries are already being made, including how much was changed to comply with certain countries' rules. These have included a menu being removed and an inconspicuous pair of underwear being added to Jason.

The first reports of Japanese censorship surfaced soon after the game was released in the country. News aggregator Genki shared a screenshot of one of the game's protagonists, Jason Duval, in the shower. In the uncensored version, Jason is naked, with his full buttcrack on show. In the Japanese version, he's wearing a cute black thong, hiding his modesty. This is relatively mild compared to the other change, though.

Unlike many versions of the game, Japanese players are unable to customize Grand Theft Auto 6's blood and gore. GameSpark, a Japanese news outlet, ran a comparison between the game's settings in and out of the country.

In the Western version, players can toggle dismemberment and change the Blood and Gore intensity on a scale of 1 (Low) to 3 (High). In the Japanese version, the Blood and Gore intensity slider has been removed, and it's assumed that Japanese players will permanently play the game with that setting at Low. Dismemberment remains, but it's far less aggressive and visceral in this version.

It puts non-English-speaking players in a tricky situation. Typically, those who wanted the full, uncensored experience could just change their PlayStation Store location and buy a copy from there. Unfortunately, the English-language versions of the game don't offer Japanese subtitles, so unless players are fluent in a second language, it becomes difficult.

The game has been out for less than 24 hours in the country, and even less in other parts of the world, so more censored scenes are likely to be discovered, but it already looks like there are some hefty cuts.

La Serie A vuole i soldi delle scommesse. Ma quanto potrebbe incassare davvero?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Il grande tema è quello del betting, cioè di avere una quota dei ricavi da scommesse”. Ezio Simonelli è tornato sul tema ieri, durante l'RCS Sport Industry Talk. Il presidente della Lega Serie A rivendica una parte degli introiti generati da un mercato che utilizza le partite di calcio come prodotto commerciale. E chiede una maggiore tutela dei dati ufficiali: “Noi questi dati li vendiamo alle agenzie di scommesse, ma qualche volta vengono venduti anche da concessionarie non ufficiali”. La questione, ovviamente, è soprattutto economica. Secondo il ReportCalcio 2025 della Figc, nel 2024 gli italiani hanno scommesso 16,1 miliardi di euro sul calcio, quasi 3 dei quali sulla sola Serie A. Su scala mondiale, il massimo campionato italiano ha generato una raccolta stimata in circa 34 miliardi di euro.

Per capire quanto potrebbe guadagnare la Lega, un riferimento interessante arriva dalla Francia. Qui esiste il cosiddetto droit au pari, che riconosce agli organizzatori delle competizioni una remunerazione sulle puntate. La Ligue de Football Professionnel ha applicato storicamente una percentuale dell'1%, chiedendone nel 2025 l'aumento al 2%. Applicando per ipotesi il modello francese ai quasi 3 miliardi raccolti in Italia sulla Serie A (che può chiedere i diritti sulle proprie competizioni, non su quelle organizzato per esempio dalla UEFA), si otterrebbero circa 30 milioni di euro all'anno, che diventerebbero 60 milioni con un'aliquota del 2%. Nel 2025, secondo i dati dell'Agenzia delle dogane e dei monopoli, le scommesse sportive a quota fissa hanno prodotto in Italia 19,2 miliardi di raccolta, ma la spesa effettiva, cioè la differenza tra puntate e vincite, è stata di 2,7 miliardi. Una percentuale calcolata sui ricavi degli operatori, anziché sulle puntate, produrrebbe dunque cifre molto inferiori.

Resta poi il problema del mercato internazionale: quei 34 miliardi di scommesse sulla Serie A comprendono Paesi con regolamentazioni differenti, nei quali non sarebbe possibile introdurre automaticamente un prelievo a favore della Lega italiana. Per questo, almeno nell'immediato, la strada più concreta potrebbe essere quella dei diritti sui dati ufficiali. La Lega già li commercializza e ha lanciato la campagna “True Data” per contrastarne l'utilizzo non autorizzato. Una fonte di ricavi distinta dalla partecipazione alle scommesse, che richiederebbe invece un intervento normativo.

BTCPay Server issues emergency update for 5 security flaws

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The EU’s cybersecurity service said the flaws could let attackers to run malicious code remotely on vulnerable servers without logging in.

BTCPay Server issued an emergency update to fix five security flaws in Nginx, urging operators of its self-hosted Bitcoin payment software to install it as soon as possible.

The vulnerabilities affect Nginx, the software that routes incoming web traffic to BTCPay’s payment application. The developers behind the Bitcoin payment software said the update installs Nginx version 1.31.9.

According to a Wednesday advisory from the European Union’s cybersecurity response team, CERT-EU, the flaws could allow attackers to remotely run malicious code on vulnerable servers without logging in.

Researchers demonstrated how to exploit one vulnerability, but CERT-EU said no attacks involving any of the five flaws were reported as of Wednesday.

BTCPay said operators can install the fix through the maintenance settings or their Docker installation. Those using node platforms should update when their platforms make it available. The project also pointed operators unable to update immediately to a temporary workaround.

The emergency update follows two earlier BTCPay Server security incidents involving a separate vulnerability that attackers exploited to steal crypto.

In August, attackers exploited a flaw to obtain credentials for Lightning Network nodes and steal funds. In November, BTCPay reported Bitcoin and Litecoin hot-wallet thefts on outdated software, saying attackers had used a different method to exploit the August flaw.

Obsbot’s New Tail 2 ADV PTZR Camera Pairs 21x Optical Zoom With AI Tracking

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Obsbot’s New Tail 2 ADV PTZR Camera Pairs 21x Optical Zoom With AI Tracking

Obsbot has announced the Tail 2 ADV, a new 4K PTZR camera featuring a 21x optical zoom and custom LUT support.

The camera, which has already earned a 2027 CES Innovation Award, features a custom Type 1/2 image sensor with Dual Native ISO and support for 4Kp60 and 1080p120 recording.

Obsbot says the camera’s AI-powered image signal processing pipeline handles noise reduction and enables 4K HDR video at up to 30p with all-pixel phase-detect autofocus.

“All-pixel PDAF and Focus Over Zooming help the camera quickly refocus on distant, stationary subjects as focal length changes during a zoom,” Obsbot says.

Speaking of zooming, the Tail 2 ADV’s lens has an equivalent focal length range of 34-702mm and a variable aperture system. At the wide end, the aperture ranges from f/1.7 to f/7, while for telephoto shooting, it ranges from f/3.8 to f/7.

In Pro mode, camera operators can remotely adjust aperture, ISO, and shutter speed, offering greater creative control in changing lighting conditions such as stage performances, outdoor productions, and houses of worship.

The camera includes built-in subject recognition for autofocus tracking, identifying people, more than 30 animal species, and over 200 object categories. Using its pan, tilt, and zoom capabilities, it can automatically adjust framing to keep moving subjects in view. Operators can choose from six tracking speed settings alongside custom modes, as well as define preferred framing and tracking boundaries.

“These settings allow live production teams to determine not just which subject the camera follows, but how it executes the shot,” Obsbot explains. The camera can also roll thanks to its built-in gimbal, enabling native 4Kp60 vertical recording without cropping.

For professional workflows, the Tail 2 ADV offers Obsbot Log—a proprietary logarithmic image profile that promises greater flexibility during color grading—alongside custom LUT support. Users can import and preview custom LUTs in Obsbot’s accompanying software, and the camera supports timecode and Precision Time Protocol (PTP) to sync with other cameras.

For connectivity, the Obsbot Tail 2 ADV includes 3G-SDI output up to 1080p60 and a full-size HDMI 2.0 output supporting up to 4K resolution. It also features Gigabit Ethernet, a 2.5mm RS-232 port, two USB-C ports, separate 3.5mm mic and line inputs, and a microSD card slot. The camera’s built-in 5,000mAh battery delivers over four and a half hours of operation, while USB PD 3.0 and PoE+ support allow for extended continuous power.

The Obsbot Tail 2 ADV PTZR camera will arrive on December 8. Preorders are open now with a limited-time $200 discount, bringing the price down from its $1,599 MSRP to $1,399.

Netflix's Newest Miniseries 'The Altruists' Tricks You Into Thinking It's Good | Review

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Why do we love watching bad people do bad things? Well, because we love watching them get their comeuppance. It's fun to be bad, but deep down, we all want crimes to be punished. We also love watching actors play pretend, especially when the subject is an infamous figure with distinct and unique mannerisms. We watched The Dropout to see Elizabeth Holmes taken down, for instance, and we loved it because of Amanda Seyfried.

The formula for these miniseries is easy enough, so when someone tampers with it, the result can either be a huge win or a massive flop. Graham Moore's The Altruists sits somewhere in the middle. Netflix's latest miniseries, starring Emmy winner Julia Garner and Olivier Award winner Anthony Boyle as crypto scammers Caroline Ellison and Sam Bankman-Fried, is a riveting portrayal, not of a pair of fraudsters running a scam, but of a toxic relationship in collapse. If it works for you, it's because you're invested in a story about the lies we tell each other and, more importantly, ourselves. If it doesn't, it's because you find everyone involved completely unbearable. Perhaps, like the show, you, too, will sit in the middle.

The Altruists chronicles the years-long, on-again, off-again romantic relationship between Sam Bankman-Fried (Boyle) and Caroline Ellison (Garner). They first meet while working at Jane Street Capital, then quit their positions and start the crypto company that eventually becomes FTX. Over the years, they struggle to define their relationship and confess their mutual feelings. This personal dilemma also spills over and eventually comes to define their business, as does their staunch support for effective altruism, a social movement in which they pledge to donate 99% of EA's profits to charity. Eventually, mishandling of their assets, inexperience, and pride lead them to be accused of stealing $8 billion of their customers' money.

The Altruists' ensemble cast also includes Alex Lawther as Sam Trabucco, co-CEO of FTX's crypto trading firm, Alameda Research; Matt Rife as Ryan Salame, CEO of FTX Digital Markets; Madison Hu as Constance Wang, FTX's COO; Karan Soni as Nishad Singh, FTX's head of engineering; Eugene Young as Gary Wang, FTX's CTO; and Paul Reiser and Robin Weigert as Joe Bankman and Barbara Fried, Sam's parents. Graham Moore serves as writer, creator, showrunner, director, and EP, alongside Jacqueline Hoyt as writer, showrunner, and EP.

When it comes to these types of stories, a fictional depiction can either humanize its subjects or satirize them. The Altruists embraces a third choice: caricature. In real life, Ellison and Bankman-Fried are already absurd enough: she's a Potterhead who enjoyed writing fanfiction that may or may not have been erotic in nature, and he's a man-child with a high-pitched voice and Fraggle Rock hair. The Altruists takes those traits and cranks them up to eleven.

Boyle, doing his best Jesse Eisenberg impression, is the single most punchable character on TV this year. Utterly deluded and deeply unpleasant, Boyle's Bankman-Fried is exactly what you'd think a crypto bro would be, so enamored with his own brain that he becomes genuinely convinced he's "the good guy." It's a deeply pathetic performance, and Boyle goes all in on it. Garner looks nothing like the real-life Ellison, so the show chooses to put distracting and unconvincing nose and teeth prosthetics on her and turn her into Holly Hunter. Alongside her adoption of a baby voice, Garner's off to the races as the awkward devil on Bankman-Fried's shoulder.

It works. Both Boyle and Garner are playing these two people in the broadest strokes possible, but boy, do they sell it. It does take a while to get used to their performances — because, again, these are two profoundly unlikable people — but somewhere past the fourth episode, when The Altruists is getting into the thick of the crime and how it happened, you suddenly find yourself enthralled by the awfulness. Garner is particularly effective in the latter half, and the penultimate episode has a scene so painfully awkward, it borders on truly chilling (based on real-life events, too). It's a moment that will surely be played at awards ceremonies, and that level of recognition for her would not be undeserved.

Everyone involved has stated that The Altruists is not a crime story, but rather a story about a relationship. This is certainly a choice for a show about two thieves who stole $8 billion from gullible people. Indeed, the press didn't romanticize their "Millennial Bonnie and Clyde" status to generate clicks; their theft was story enough. Yet, The Altruists frames everything in the context of Sam and Caroline's relationship. Every major choice, development, and consequence is a direct response to their incredibly toxic, co-dependent, and ultimately frustrating dynamic.

As a result, The Altruists implicitly excuses the story's leads. It's not like they wanted to steal the money; they were just too in love to see the bigger picture. Don't get me wrong, it's still a painfully unflattering portrayal. Here, Bankman-Fried is, for lack of a better word, a loser who becomes so consumed by the one girl who paid attention to him that he tanked a multi-billion-dollar business because of it. For her part, Caroline is a force of destruction, encouraging Sam's worst tendencies while remaining firmly convinced that he's the one doing the bad things, not her.

All this makes for good television, even while you remain constantly aware of the lie being sold. It's not like viewers tune in to these crime shows expecting a factual documentary, but they're often more willing to buy into a specific version of events. The Altruists is less a drama than a soap opera, with everything from lies to fake break-ups, will-they-won't-they dynamics, and even LARPing. It wants you to be more interested in the story's central duo than their crimes — but when those crimes are so serious, and the relationship is so aggravating, why would you?

The Altruists is a story about the most selfish kind of selflessness, the type born from ego rather than empathy. It's also a story about stunted growth and children playing at being adults: they drink colorful drinks with funny umbrellas, keep crucial information on their PS5s, and crave their parents' approval more than anything. Who cares if they stole $8 billion? Who cares if the world is on fire and they've poured the gasoline? The girl Sam loves says she loves him back.

And The Altruists almost buys into this delusion: maybe these two were really good guys. Maybe they did want to change the world. Maybe things just got out of hand. Perhaps that's Bankman-Fried and Ellison's last great trick: conning viewers into thinking their story is more interesting and more dramatic than it actually is. That they are special enough to warrant our attention, that their romance was unique enough to deserve a show, that they will forever live in pop culture infamy. For the length of a miniseries, it mostly works. In the end, though, once the final credits roll, you can see that, much like the missing $8 billion from FTX, all that's there is a hole, and no number of episodes can fill it.

The Altruists is now streaming on Netflix.

The Altruists wants you to care more about the romance between Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison than their crime.

The Oura Ring 5 Is On Sale for the First Time Ahead of Black Friday

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

THE OURA RING has been my go-to daily fitness tracker for almost a decade, and for Black Friday, Oura is offering a massive discount on their latest model. Also, heads up: if you have an FSA or HSA plan, Oura Ring 5 is covered.

Here is everything you need to know about the Oura Ring Black Friday and Cyber Monday sale.

The Oura Ring 5 is Oura's latest offering, and a few upgrades earned it a spot on our Best Smart Rings list. This version has no noticeable sensors, while previous models had three interior nodes that could be a bit uncomfortable. It still collects health data seamlessly.

The Oura Ring 5 is lighter and slimmer than any previous models. The ring provides real-time stress tracking, continuous heart-rate monitoring, and more precise temperature sensing. All of this is designed to give you more precise, helpful feedback throughout the day. The ring’s interior has also changed from an epoxy to all-over titanium for a lighter feel.

This means you can keep an extra charge for your ring while you're traveling or on the move so you don't miss out on any insights. The new charging dock is also improved and can charge your ring in about an hour.

Oura recommends wearing the ring on your forefinger, since that's where your pulse is strongest, which can mean slightly better data, but really any finger works. I know a few people who use their Oura Ring as their wedding band. So if you know your ring finger size you’re good to go on that.

If you're unsure about your size, it could be a good idea to order one of Oura's sizing kits. It’s now $10 on Amazon, and that gets applied to your future ring purchase, so technically it's "free." And it allows you to lock in the sale.

When you purchase on ouraring.com the process of getting a sizing kit is a little more involved. You order the ring first, and then they send you a sizing kit to use to get your correct size in the system. Depending on which route you choose for the sizing kit, you'll use that same route to purchase your ring when you decide to buy.

If you want to make sure you lock in the sale, you can purchase the size you feel is right, and as long as you return it within 30 days, you can resize after. You can find all of the details on those returns and exchanges on their site.

You might not be looking for a super-slim smart ring. If you’re used to wearing rings with some weight, or you just like something with a little more heft, the Oura Ring 4 is the first version I think is worth keeping, even if you’re buying a new Oura Ring 5.

‘The Altruists’ Review: Netflix FTX Series Fails to Get a Handle on Crypto Scammer Sam Bankman-Fried

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The collapse of cryptocurrency exchange FTX in 2022 — a spectacular implosion that saw the company evaporate and sent founder Sam Bankman-Fried to prison, along with much of his inner circle — felt epic in the moment. But compared to the frenzy around artificial intelligence that was soon to come, even the Shakespearean downfall of a corporation that could once casually spend nine figures on stadium naming rights feels like a footnote in the intertwined history of technology and finance. The crypto boom now occupies an awkward middle space in the recent arc of the tech industry: After the Obama-era idealism of Facebook, Twitter and other companies that promised to connect the world for the better and before the full-on dystopian rhetoric of post-human labor and a “permanent underclass.” Related Stories How to Watch the CMA Awards (And Taylor Swift's Performance) Live Online 'Grendel' Animated Series Set at Netflix From 'Widow's Bay' Writer Years After Live-Action Adaptation Was Scrapped

The Netflix limited series “The Altruists,” created by Graham Moore (the Oscar-winning screenwriter of “The Imitation Game”) and produced by the Obamas’ shingle Higher Ground, is aware of this gap. “AI is hotter than crypto’s ever been right now,” one character observes in the finale as FTX’s house of cards starts to fall. (Marquee frontier lab and FTX investee Anthropic even makes a cameo.) “The Altruists” could theoretically use our rapidly fading cultural memory to its advantage, dusting off the Bankman-Fried story to deliver a character study that pushes past tabloid headlines about polycules and party mansions to make sense of what happened and a case for why it mattered.

Instead, “The Altruists” opts for the most conventional, least persuasive lens through which to explain the significance of FTX as well as what went wrong: the on-and-off romance between Bankman-Fried (Irish actor Anthony Boyle) and Caroline Ellison (Julia Garner, also an executive producer), the former head of FTX-affiliated hedge fund Alameda Research. The action toggles back and forth between two timelines: the early days of Sam, Caroline and their companies and their swift demise five years later, forecasted in a standard flash-forward to the FBI storming the office.

The erstwhile couple make a certain sense as the show’s focus. They’re a handy metaphor for the messy, inappropriate relationship between their two companies, which culminated in FTX’s use of billions in customer money to cover for Alameda’s trading losses. And, of course, there are salacious details like Ellison’s X-rated “Harry Potter” fanfiction — the kind of low-hanging fruit a writer would have to be foolish to leave on the vine. Sex still sells, even when the sex in question is between socially awkward math geeks whose idea of foreplay is strategic board games.

But “The Altruists,” which works from a series of New York magazine articles by Kevin T. Dugan and Jen Wieczner as its source material, makes Sam and Caroline strangely simplistic, both as individual personalities and larger cultural forces. It starts with the casting: Boyle is a strong actor with a bright future ahead of him; the onetime face of a brash IRA revolutionary in “Say Nothing” is nonetheless an awkward fit for a noted oddball like Bankman-Fried, and Boyle never disappears into the role. His SBF — the boy billionaire’s popular nickname — is motormouthed and visibly anxious, yet the scripts elide some of his strangest tics, like taking interviews while playing video games or sleeping on the office floor. (The bean bag habit is alluded to in dialogue, yet never shown. An odd choice for a visual medium!) Boyle’s performance comes off more like the Mark Zuckerberg of “The Social Network,” a disaffected yet basically relatable nerd who craves validation, than the Mark Zuckerberg of “The Social Reckoning,” a space alien whose inability to grasp human nature has warped the world in his image.

Garner, for her part, dials up Ellison’s tics into a near-caricature of a four-eyed poindexter. Pre-arrest footage of Ellison, who would go on to cooperate extensively with SBF’s prosecutors, shows the same nervous laughter that Garner accurately mimics, but a much more calm and even speaking voice. Amping up an accent and donning a wig are old hat for the star of “Inventing Anna,” another frustrating Netflix project that captured its subject’s eccentricity without delving into their deeper psychology. Were “The Altruists” more of a straight-up satire in tone, Garner’s caricature might work in context. Instead, isolated moments of humor — upon finding out about the fraud, one FTX employee is mad because he’s been urging them to break the law for years and feels excluded — are left to float in an otherwise sincere story of misguided puppy love. (In a striking detail seemingly invented for the show, Caroline is the first woman Sam ever sleeps with.) Perhaps Moore and co-showrunner Jacqueline Hoyt were worried about crossing over into gleeful cruelty. But if perpetrators of a multibillion dollar fraud don’t deserve some mockery, who does?

Sam and Caroline are initially drawn together as young analysts at the Wall Street firm Jane Street Capital by their mutual interest in effective altruism, the “earn to give” movement that gives “The Altruists” its name. (Characters affiliated with EA are frequently identified by their branded T-shirts.) The two encourage each other’s most dangerous instincts, like going all in on every bet because it’s “mathematically optimal” or refusing to communicate discomfort around Sam’s autocratic tendencies or Caroline’s polyamory. The plot frames Sam and Caroline’s slide into criminality as the slow corruption of self-identified “good guys,” though the show is strangely uncritical of the starting premise that the best way to make positive change in the world is by amassing a personal fortune.

For all EA comes into focus, cryptocurrency itself remains stubbornly obscure. It’s not easy to mine drama from complex financial instruments, but “The Altruists” puts frustratingly little effort into explaining the water these crazy kids are swimming in. Without a more defined sense of what makes crypto unique, “The Altruists” is left with a faint rehash of the techno-optimism-gone-awry narrative already explored in series like “The Dropout,” “Super Pumped” and “WeCrashed,” which tellingly dropped within weeks of each other in 2022 (and just a few months before FTX flamed out). Those three titles collectively worked to capture a specific time and place; with its personal focus, “The Altruists” never does the same.

“The Altruists” even gestures at more productive angles on the whole FTX affair than the folie a deux of Sam and Caroline. Potential framing devices, like a Bloomberg reporter’s investigation, are flirted with and abandoned. Nishad Singh (Karan Soni), SBF’s childhood friend turned employee, tells us that Sam’s Stanford Law professor parents Joe (Paul Reiser) and Barbara (Robin Weigert) are the skeleton key to his need to prove himself. “They already love you,” Nishad says of the scholars. “They just don’t fucking like you.” It’s a great line, just not one backed up in any way by the show’s portrayal of that relationship, which is fairly minimal and fails to lay the groundwork for head-scratching decisions like Joe and Barbara’s total credulity toward their son, both before and after his arrest.

It’s not like “The Altruists” lacks time to explore the Bankman-Fried household dynamics. (The resources to convincingly render far-flung locations like Macau and Malta do, however, seem scarce.) At eight hourlong episodes, “The Altruists” is padded out with detours into Nishad’s office romance and the backstory of SBF’s college pal Sam Trabucco (Alex Lawther), who left FTX before things hit the fan. The space would be far better spent building out the crypto universe, shading in Sam and Caroline outside one another’s orbit or even outlandish incidents like FTX’s Larry David-starring Super Bowl ad. Without a stronger center, though, these satellite plots feel like they’re orbiting a vacuum.

All eight episodes of “The Altruists” are now streaming on Netflix.

‘The Altruists’ Review: Anthony Boyle and Julia Garner Make a Toxically Perfect Match in Netflix’s Intriguing but Unsatisfying Crypto Love Story

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Based on the real-life rise and fall of FTX, the eight-part miniseries from Graham Moore ('The Imitation Game') delves into the romance between founder Sam Bankman-Fried and exec Caroline Ellison.

As FTX founder Sam Bankman-Fried (Anthony Boyle) begins his very public downfall, one salacious detail starts to go viral: That Caroline Ellison (Julia Garner), CEO of sister firm Alameda and his ex-girlfriend, was a prolific writer of smutty Harry Potter fanfiction.

The fact that her private hobby has nothing to do with the financial crimes they’re in hot water for is beside the point. “What gets more clicks than a complicated financial scandal? A young professional woman who’s into kink,” shrugs Constance (Madison Hu), FTX’s loyal-to-the-end COO. “I mean, that’s the story. The whole world didn’t care about Caroline until they realized that she was horny.” Related Stories TV Netflix's New Releases Coming in November 2026 TV 'The Altruists': Anthony Boyle's Sam Bankman-Fried Caught in Crypto Crisis in Netflix Series Trailer The Altruists The Bottom Line All heart and not enough head. Airdate: Thursday, Nov. 19 (Netflix)Cast: Julia Garner, Anthony Boyle, Alex Lawther, Karan Soni, Madison Hu, Eugene Young, Naomi Okada, Matt Rife, Robin Weigert, Paul Reiser, Terry ChenCreator: Graham Moore

Sam, who knows better than anyone how mortified Caroline must be, seems troubled by the notion. But it more or less appears to be the impulse driving Netflix’s The Altruists. The eight-hour series chronicles the rise and fall of FTX and Alameda through the tumultuous relationship between its leaders — and in doing so, deepens the saga’s emotional relatability while also diminishing its stakes.

For most of the season, creator Graham Moore (The Imitation Game) cuts between two timelines. In the halcyon days of 2016, captured by director James Ponsoldt in the warm bright tones and slightly grainy texture of an indie rom-com, Caroline meets Sam on her first day at a New York hedge fund. The two recognize each other almost immediately as kindred spirits, matched in their intelligence (high), their interests (math and winning) and their taste in jokes (charmingly dorky).

In no time, they’re falling into bed, though not quite into a relationship — Caroline prefers to keep it caszh, reasoning that non-monogamy is more “mathematically optimal” for getting off. Instead, they tie their fates together via their careers. Sam first founds Alameda and then FTX, and in short order brings Caroline on board as an exec.

The businesses are rooted in the couple’s shared principle of effective altruism. In theory, EA is a rationalist moral framework that prioritizes doing the greatest good. In practice, like most guiding philosophies, it’s easily twisted into whatever a self-interested devotee wants it to be.

Betraying a colleague, conning oblivious business partners, stealing customer money — why, any of it might be justifiable, noble even, so long as it’s done with the intention of making more money to definitely for sure donate to some worthy cause someday. And so it is that in the show’s other, grayer timeline, it’s 2022 and Sam and Caroline find themselves scrambling to cover up all the grave financial misdeeds they’ve committed in service of some vague higher purpose.

How one timeline could lead into the other is The Altruists’ central concern, though the answer isn’t really much mystery (and not only because it was all headline news a scant four years ago). The differences between the Caroline and Sam we meet in 2016 and the ones we’ll part with in 2022 are ones of degree, not kind. These two have always been aggressive about taking risks, arrogant about their ability to outplay them, skeptical of the rules and gifted in justifying their own bullshit to themselves as perfectly rational, and therefore objectively correct, decisions.

Add in the fact that they tend to augment each other’s worst impulses — backing each other’s wildest moves, one-upping each other for spite or for fun, treating every office disagreement like a proxy for the conversations about commitment or exclusivity they’re supposedly too evolved to need — and FTX/Alameda’s meltdown starts to look almost inevitable. As ex-colleague and ex-friend Trabucco (a very good Alex Lawther) puts it, “Sam was a block of sodium. Caroline was a rushing river.” Put those ingredients together, the MIT graduate explains, and you’ve got an instant fireball.

If the journey contains few surprises, however, it does offer some worthy features. Karan Soni brings much-needed sweetness as FTX engineer Nishad, whose cute will-they-won’t-they with a coworker (Naomi Okada) becomes collateral damage in the implosion. Paul Reiser and Robin Weigert are fun in supporting roles as Sam’s parents, though their appearances are so brief that another character has to explain that while they love their son, they don’t like him. On the other end of the lovability spectrum, Terry Chen drinks up the part of possibly mob-affiliated rival crypto founder CZ with as much gusto as his character does his fancy health tonics.

The real (vegan) meat of the story, of course, is the bond between Sam and Caroline. That, the series understands inside and out. Boyle disappears into the part of Sam, whose boyish arrogance is somehow both his most alluring quality and his most repugnant. He’s outdone only slightly by the chameleonic Garner, who plays the mousy but mighty Caroline like she might see all the gears turning just underneath the surface of reality if she only stares hard enough.

When the two are across a room from one another, even editing a spreadsheet can turn into an act of thrilling flirtation. I hesitate to describe their chemistry as irresistible only because that might suggest we want to see them together. In actuality, the questionably effective but not entirely unpleasant experience of watching The Altruists is to simultaneously believe with your whole heart that these crazy kids are meant for each other and that we’d all have been better off if they’d never met.

The show’s intense focus on their love story distinguishes it, somewhat, from the increasingly crowded field of dramas about scammy and scummy tech founders. The usual rise-fall arc is emotionally complicated by the ménage à trois between Sam, Caroline and FTX/Alameda: The lovers might insist they always put the work first, but The Altruists know well how vulnerable even billionaire geniuses can be to whims of their hearts or loins. (Ahem.)

But as a way into what is still ultimately the story of a tech scam, the romantic angle doesn’t entirely work. Amid all the whirlwind drama, a sense of perspective gets lost. The number $8 billion, as in the amount of money FTX has stolen from its customers, gets lobbed about in every episode. But who those clients are, what impact the crime has in the larger world, what cultural forces allowed SBF’s FTX to get so big so fast to begin with, what consequences are left in the wake of its collapse — all these factors are barely mentioned at all.

You come away with the sense that the real lesson of the whole FTX debacle is what a tragedy it is when two people so intelligent are too caught up in their own heads to simply say what’s in their hearts, to the point that it’s easier to perpetrate financial crimes on a historic scale than it is to simply say “I love you.” In a vacuum, I suppose it’s a worthy lesson. In a broader context, however, it seems an unreasonably expensive one.

Hearst Canvas Launches Frontier Collective Brand as U.K. Arm Builds Digital-Native Content Slate for YouTube

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hearst Canvas, the creator-led media arm of U.K.-based Hearst Networks, has launched its latest digital native brand with Frontier Collective, an effort to harness the YouTube and social media followings of top outdoor adventure content creators.

The effort has quietly bowed over the past few weeks starting with a roundtable-style talk show, “Expedition HQ,” featuring four prominent U.K. explorers at the core of Frontier Collective: Aldo Kane, Alex Bescoby, Levison Wood and Lucy Shepherd. The foursome come together swap stories about explorations and derring-do in their efforts to explore the natural world and far-flung exotic locales. “Expedition HQ” bowed Nov. 1 on the Frontier Collective YouTube channel, with new episodes released weekly. Clips are spread widely around Instagram, TikTok, Facebook and Snapchat. Related Stories Michelle Monaghan and Justin Theroux to Star in ‘Sleepover’ From Blumhouse Atomic and Universal 'Bad Boys for Life' Directors Adil El Arbi and Bilall Fallah Invest in 'Sound of Metal' Producer Caviar (EXCLUSIVE)

Hearst Canvas hopes to tap into the heat of the adventure content community on YouTube. The Hearst Canvas division is set up to find top-tier creators who can contribute to strong verticals as Hearst Networks develops new content and formats designed to roll out on YouTube. Dean Possenniskie, managing director, Hearst Networks, Europe, Middle East and Africa and managing director Hearst Networks U.K., is overseeing the Canvas venture that was launched in October 2025.

“We’ve now built a dedicated creative team that is going to originate formats in a different style that are designed to work as creator-led content on YouTube,” Possenniskie told Variety. “A format like Frontier Collective is unique in the fact it brings together four very strong talents in the space. It’s not an audience that we traditionally reach here in the U.K. through pay TV or DTC or broadcast channels. It’s a global audience, and it’s a much broader audience for us to reach now as well. So that’s a really exciting opportunity.”

Hearst Canvas is headed by Danny Baggot as creative director and Sam Pearson as executive producer. The pair unveiled the venture’s first commission on Oct. 29, the unscripted business revitalization series “Pitch, Please.”

“Frontier Collective offers a compelling opportunity to build something unique in the adventure space — a network of remarkable individuals whose passion drives them to explore the far reaches of our globe and share what they’ve experienced with the world,” Pearson said. “And with ‘Expedition HQ,’ we’re launching a returning format that delivers on this promise through fascinating and insightful conversations.”

The roundtable series is produced by Grammar Productions, co-founded by Bescoby. It’s produced shows for BBC, Channel 4, Apple TV, Amazon Prime, Discovery, Sky HISTORY and Canal+, in addition to custom work for brands.

As a veteran of the adventure genre, Bescoby said he sees demand for Frontier Collective as a concept.

“Some of the best adventure storytellers I know have spent years asking for a proper home for their work,” Bescoby said. “Traditional television has stepped back from the genre, even as audiences are clamoring for more. It’s hugely exciting that Hearst Canvas is building that home with Frontier Collective, and it’s amazing to be a part of it.”

Kane invoked the importance of the YouTube channel as serving as a hub for adventure content fan communities. “This new YouTube channel allows us to bring directly to our community the real story that goes on behind the scenes on these adventures,” he said.

Wood added that every major adventure yields its own unique experiences, and inevitably, there is more color than can be included in the final program. “So much never makes it into the finished documentaries. I’m looking forward to sharing more of those experiences and unseen footage on my own channels as part of Frontier Collective,” Wood said.

Shepherd added her enthusiasm for the push to build a hub for top digital-native explorers and adventurers.

“I’m only sorry this didn’t begin sooner. There’s something very special about sitting around a table with people whose adventures inspired me to set out on my own,” Shepherd said. “Life is an expedition, and I hope our stories inspire others to find a little adventure in their lives, wherever they are.”

(Pictured: “Expedition HQ” hosts Alex Bescoby, Lucy Shepherd, Levison Wood and Aldo Kane)

Leeds United marksman Dominic Calvert-Lewin is in the wrong international era, according to former England forward Mark Hateley.

Calvert-Lewin returned to the England fold earlier this year and his scintillating club form has pushed his caps tally to 15.

He currently sits at the chief understudy to Harry Kane, who was recently crowned winner of the coveted Ballon d’Or award.

Hateley, who won 32 England caps between 1984 and 1992 and had a loan spell at Leeds in 1996, has rued the timing of Calvert-Lewin’s emergence on the international scene.

He told The Yorkshire Post via London.Bet: “I'm going to say the only way that Calvert-Lewin will get a game is if he's (Kane) injured, because I think he will be first in. He's got to play the long game. Harry's going to play for a long time, I think.

“Sports science and everything going forward is all geared for playing longer. The way Harry lives his life, his lifestyle, his ability to adapt as a player... Calvert-Lewin is in the wrong place at the wrong time, wrong era.

“He's just got himself stuck behind an absolute legend of a player that just seems to get better and better and better. He'll (Kane) crush 150 plus games, the goal tally will go up and up and up. That's who you've just got to sit behind, but you've got to be ready.”

Calvert-Lewin may not be England’s number nine but he is certainly the chief marksman at Elland Road. He has enjoyed a career rejuvenation in West Yorkshire, notching 20 goals across 51 appearances since his free transfer move from Everton in the summer of 2025.

Hateley said: “You've got to be doing your stuff at Leeds, you've got to be scoring goals at Leeds, staying fit, playing well.

“I think the problem with Calvert-Lewin is, in his younger career, there was a lot of niggly injuries. A lot of setbacks, holding him back, not being able to get on runs.

“When you're being selected for an international set-up, you have to be consistently on top of your game. You're persistently good. You're always there knocking at the door. That's what Calvert-Lewin's got to do now.

“He's got to keep himself fit. He's got to do good things with Leeds and score goals. It's as simple as that.”

Trump's massive DC triumphal arch project dealt another blow by federal judge

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A federal judge extended the construction halt on President Donald Trump's proposed 250-foot triumphal arch in Washington, D.C., until at least Dec. 3, prompting criticism from the White House amid ongoing legal challenges.

President Donald Trump's proposed 250-foot triumphal arch near Arlington in Washington, D.C., was dealt another blow Wednesday when a federal judge extended an order blocking construction until at least early December.

U.S. District Judge Tanya Chutkan extended her initial Nov. 5 temporary restraining order blocking construction of the arch for another 14 days, delaying progress on the structure until at least Dec. 3.

The extension comes as Chutkan's original order was set to expire Thursday.

The judge said the extension would give her additional time to consider lawsuits filed by the District of Columbia and Virginia seeking a longer-term injunction against the project.

MULTIPLE KENNEDY FAMILY MEMBERS SEEK TO JOIN LEGAL BATTLE AGAINST TRUMP'S TRIUMPHAL ARCH

President Donald Trump's proposed 250-foot triumphal arch in Washington, D.C., faces another legal setback after a federal judge extended an order blocking construction until at least Dec. 3. (Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images)

The order marks the latest setback for one of the president's major projects in the nation's capital. Inspired by Paris' Arc de Triomphe, the proposed monument would stand near the Memorial Bridge, which connects Arlington National Cemetery to Washington, D.C.

In a statement to Fox News Digital, the White House criticized the order, saying the project was being undercut by an "activist district court."

"The judiciary should not be abused to politicize a structure designed to unite our nation," White House spokesman Davis Ingle said.

"We will continue making the case for this beautiful and fitting monument and are confident we will be fully vindicated as this litigation proceeds," he added.

EXCAVATION SET TO BEGIN ON TRUMP ADMINISTRATION'S 250-FOOT TRIUMPHAL ARCH AMID LEGAL BATTLE

Above is the updated rendering of the proposed United States Triumphal Arch, showing the 250-foot monument without the four lion statues that appeared in earlier designs. (Harrison Design)

Chutkan issued the temporary restraining order earlier this month just hours after the National Capital Planning Commission approved the project.

The approval had cleared the way for construction to begin before the court intervened.

Trump has previously said the triumphal arch would be converted into a military complex capable of rapidly deploying large numbers of drones, with snipers positioned on the roof and plaza areas.

He also said the complex would store "large quantities of sniper ammunition."

The project has drawn criticism from preservationists, lawmakers and veterans, who argue that it could disrupt views of the Lincoln Memorial and Arlington National Cemetery.

A view of Columbia Island, the site of President Donald Trump's proposed 250-foot triumphal arch between the Lincoln Memorial and Arlington Cemetery. (Aaron Schwartz/Getty Images)

Last week, nine members of the Kennedy family also sought to stop the project, saying it would obstruct views and cast shadows over their relatives' grave sites at Arlington National Cemetery.

Lo ha ribadito in maniera ancora più chiara: "Sono disponibile a vendere il Torino. Spero che arrivino proposte per dare opportunità a nuovi imprenditori". Il Presidente Urbano Cairo, nel corso del RCS Sport Industry Talk, ha aperto nuovamente alla cessione del club. In passato aveva parlato di voler passare la mano a figure più forti e più ricche di lui. Stavolta l'invito è stato più generico, strizzando l'occhio anche ai fondi di investimento stranieri. Cairo naturalmente non ha ipotizzato cifre. Ma la sua valutazione del Torino si aggira intorno ai duecento milioni. Niente che possa spaventare realtà economiche importanti decise a entrare nel mondo del calcio italiano.

L'apertura di Cairo non lascia spazio a interpretazioni. E non sembra aver il tentativo di guadagnare tempo o di voler addolcire una contestazione nei suoi confronti di una parte del tifo granata. Chi ha un progetto serio si faccia avanti. Uscendo magari anche allo scoperto. La Serie A non ha più l'appeal di un tempo. I grandi ricchi del mondo continuano a inseguire un posto in Premier. Per trovare compratori sarebbe prezioso anche un segnale da parte della squadra. La formazione di Abate per il momento è nel limbo del campionato. Naviga in undicesima posizione senza infamia e senza gloria. Servirebbe un salto in avanti anche per invogliare Cairo a fare qualche altro piccolo investimento nel mercato invernale. Il Toro è un mondo in attesa. Che aspetta solo di poter ripartire a cento all'ora accompagnando una nuova sfida
